<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--16_exchange_posthumus" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — exchange (POSTHUMUS) [16]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | (coming forward) Ay, so thou dost, Italian fiend! Ay me, most credulous [[Fool|fool]], Egregious murderer, thief, anything That’s due to all the villains past, in being, To come! O, give me cord, or knife, or [[Poison|poison]], Some upright justicer! Thou, [[King]], send out For torturers ingenious. It is I That all th‘abhorrèd things o’th’ earth amend By being worse than they. I am [[Posthumus]], That killed thy daughter—[[Villain|villain]]-like, I lie: That caused a lesser [[Villain|villain]] than myself, A sacrilegious thief, to do’t. The temple Of virtue was she; yea, and she herself. Spit and throw stones, cast mire upon me, set The [[dogs]] o‘th’ street to bay me. Every [[Villain|villain]] Be called [[Posthumus]] Leonatus, and Be ‘[[Villain|villain]]’ less than ’twas! O [[Innogen]]! My [[Queen|queen]], my life, my wife, O [[Innogen]], [[Innogen]], [[Innogen]]! |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | (approaching him) Peace, my lord. Hear, hear. |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | Shall ’s have a play of this? Thou scornful page, There lie thy part. He strikes her down |
| PISANIO | (coming forward) O gentlemen, help! Mine and your mistress! O my lord [[Posthumus]], You ne’er killed [[Innogen]] till now. Help, help! (To [[Innogen]]) Mine honoured lady. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Does the world go round? |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | How comes these staggers on me? |
| PISANIO | (to [[Innogen]]) Wake, my mistress. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | If this be so, the gods do mean to strike me To [[Death|death]] with mortal joy. |
| PISANIO | (to [[Innogen]]) How fares my mistress? |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | O, get thee from my sight! Thou gav’st me [[Poison|poison]]. Dangerous fellow, hence. Breathe not where princes are. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | The tune of [[Innogen]]. |
| PISANIO | Lady, the gods throw stones of sulphur on me if That box I gave you was not thought by me A precious thing. I had it from the [[Queen]]. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | New matter still. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | It poisoned me. |
| CORNELIUS | O gods! I left out one thing which the [[Queen]] confessed (To Pisanio) Which must approve thee honest. ’If Pisanio Have‘, said she, ‘given his mistress that confection Which I gave him for cordial, she is served As I would serve a rat.’ |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | What’s this, Cornelius? |
| CORNELIUS | The [[Queen]], sir, very oft importuned me To temper poisons for her, still pretending The satisfaction of her knowledge only In killing creatures vile, as cats and [[dogs]] Of no esteem. I, dreading that her purpose Was of more danger, did compound for her A certain stuff which, being ta‘en, would cease The present power of life, but in short [[Time|time]] All offices of [[Nature|nature]] should again Do their due functions. (To [[Innogen]]) Have you ta’en of it? |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | Most like I did, for I was dead. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | (aside to [[Guiderius]] and [[Arviragus]]) My boys, There was our error. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | This is sure Fidele. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | (to [[Posthumus]]) Why did you throw your wedded lady from you? Think that you are upon a lock, and now Throw me again. She throws her arms about his neck |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | Hang there like fruit, my [[Soul|soul]], Till the tree die. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | (to [[Innogen]]) How now, my flesh, my [[Child|child]]? What, mak’st thou me a dullard in this act? Wilt thou not speak to me? |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--17_exchange_innogen" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — exchange (INNOGEN) [17]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | (kneeling) Your blessing, sir. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | (aside to [[Guiderius]] and [[Arviragus]]) Though you did [[Love|love]] this youth, I blame ye not. You had a motive for’t. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | My [[Tears|tears]] that fall Prove holy [[Water|water]] on thee! He raises her [[Innogen]], Thy mother’s dead. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | I am sorry for’t, my lord. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | O, she was naught, and ’long of her it was That we meet here so strangely. But her son Is gone, we know not how nor where. |
| PISANIO | My lord, Now [[Fear|fear]] is from me I’ll speak [[Truth|truth]]. Lord [[Cloten]], Upon my lady’s missing, came to me With his [[Sword|sword]] drawn, foamed at the mouth, and swore If I discovered not which way she was gone It was my instant [[Death|death]]. By accident I had a feigned letter of my master’s Then in my pocket, which directed him To seek her on the [[Mountains|mountains]] near to Milford, Where in a frenzy, in my master’s garments, Which he enforced from me, away he posts With unchaste purpose, and with oath to violate My lady’s [[Honour|honour]]. What became of him I further know not. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Let me end the story. I slew him there. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Marry, the gods forfend! I would not thy good deeds should from my lips Pluck a hard sentence. Prithee, valiant youth, Deny’t again. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | I have spoke it, and I did it. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | He was a prince. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | A most incivil one. The wrongs he did me Were nothing prince-like, for he did provoke me With language that would make me spurn the sea If it could so roar to me. I cut off ’s head, And am right glad he is not standing here To tell this tale of mine. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | I am sorrow for thee. By thine own tongue thou art condemned, and must Endure our law. Thou’rt dead. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | That headless man I thought had been my lord. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | (to soldiers) Bind the offender, And take him from our presence. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Stay, sir [[King]]. This boy is better than the man he slew, As [[Well|well]] descended as thyself, and hath More of thee merited than a band of Clotens Had ever scar for. Let his arms [[Alone|alone]]; They were not born for bondage. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Why, old soldier, Wilt thou undo the worth thou art unpaid for By tasting of our wrath? How of descent As good as we? |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | In that he spake too far. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | to [[Belarius]] And thou shalt die for’t. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | We will die all three But I will prove that two on ’s are as good As I have given out him. My sons, I must For mine own part unfold a dangerous speech, Though haply [[Well|well]] for you. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | Your danger’s ours. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | And our good his. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Have at it then. By leave, Thou hadst, great [[King]], a subject who Was called [[Belarius]]. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | What of him? He is A banished traitor. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | He it is that hath Assumed this age. Indeed, a banished man; I know not how a traitor. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | (to soldiers) Take him hence. The whole world shall not save him. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Not too hot. First pay me for the nursing of thy sons, And let it be confiscate all so soon As I have received it. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--18_exchange_cymbeline" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — exchange (CYMBELINE) [18]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>

|---|---|
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Nursing of my sons? |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | I am too blunt and saucy. (Kneeling) Here’s my knee. Ere I arise I will prefer my sons, Then spare not the old father. Mighty sir, These two young gentlemen that call me father And think they are my sons are none of mine. They are the issue of your loins, my liege, And [[Blood|blood]] of your begetting. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | How, my issue? |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--19_monologue_belarius" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — monologue (BELARIUS) [19]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | So sure as you your father’s. I, old Morgan, Am that [[Belarius]] whom you sometime banished. Your pleasure was my mere offence, my punishment Itself, and all my treason. That I suffered Was all the harm I did. These gentle princes— For such and so they are-these twenty years Have I trained up. Those arts they have as I Could put into them. My breeding was, sir, As your highness knows. Their [[Nurse|nurse]] Euriphile, Whom for the theft I wedded, stole these children Upon my banishment. I moved her to‘t, Having received the punishment before For that which I did then. Beaten for loyalty Excited me to treason. Their dear loss, The more of you ‘twas felt, the more it shaped Unto my end of stealing them. But, gracious sir, Here are your sons again, and I must lose Two of the sweet’st companions in the world. The benediction of these covering heavens Fall on their heads like dew, for they are worthy To inlay heaven with [[Stars|stars]]. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--20_exchange_cymbeline" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — exchange (CYMBELINE) [20]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Thou weep‘st, and speak’st. The service that you three have done is more Unlike than this thou tell’st. I lost my children. If these be they, I know not how to wish A pair of worthier sons. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | rising Be pleased a while. This gentleman, whom I call Polydore, Most worthy prince, as yours, is true [[Guiderius]]. [[Guiderius]] kneels This gentleman, my Cadwal, [[Arviragus]], Your younger princely son. [[Arviragus]] kneels He, sir, was lapped In a most curious mantle wrought by th’ hand Of his [[Queen|queen]] mother, which for more probation I can with ease produce. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | [[Guiderius]] had Upon his neck a mole, a sanguine star. It was a mark of [[Wonder|wonder]]. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | This is he, Who hath upon him still that natural stamp. It was wise [[Nature|nature]]’s end in the donation To be his evidence now. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | O, what am I? A mother to the birth of three? Ne’er mother Rejoiced deliverance more. Blest pray you be, That, after this strange starting from your orbs, You may reign in them now! [[Guiderius]] and [[Arviragus]] rise O [[Innogen]], Thou hast lost by this a kingdom. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | No, my lord, I have got two worlds by’t. O my gentle brothers, Have we thus met? O, never say hereafter But I am truest speaker. You called me brother When I was but your sister; I you brothers When ye were so indeed. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Did you e’er meet? |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | Ay, my good lord. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | And at first meeting loved, Continued so until we thought he died. |
| CORNELIUS | By the [[Queen]]’s dram she swallowed. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | O rare instinct! When shall I hear all through? This fierce abridgement Hath to it circumstantial branches which Distinction should be rich in. Where? How lived you? And when came you to serve our Roman captive? How parted with your brothers? How first met them? Why fled you from the [[Court|court]]? And whither? These, And your three motives to the battle, with I know not how much more, should be demanded, And all the other by-dependences, From chance to chance. But nor the [[Time|time]] nor place Will serve our long inter’gatories. See, [[Posthumus]] anchors upon [[Innogen]], And she, like harmless [[Lightning|lightning]], throws her eye On him, her brothers, me, her master, hitting Each object with a joy. The counterchange Is severally in all. Let’s quit this ground, And smoke the temple with our sacrifices. (To [[Belarius]]) Thou art my brother; so we’ll hold thee ever. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | (to [[Belarius]]) You are my father too, and did relieve me To see this gracious season. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | All o’erjoyed, Save these in bonds. Let them be joyful too, For they shall taste our comfort. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | (to [[Lucius]]) My good master, I will yet do you service. |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | Happy be you! |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | The forlorn soldier that so nobly fought, He would have [[Well|well]] becomed this place, and graced The thankings of a [[King|king]]. |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | I am, sir, The soldier that did company these three In poor beseeming. ’Twas a fitment for The purpose I then followed. That I was he, Speak, Giacomo; I had you down, and might Have made you finish. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--21_exchange_giacomo" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — exchange (GIACOMO) [21]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| GIACOMO | (kneeling) I am down again, But now my heavy [[Conscience|conscience]] sinks my knee As then your force did. Take that life, beseech you, Which I so often owe; but your ring first, And here the bracelet of the truest princess That ever swore her [[Faith|faith]]. |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | (raising him) Kneel not to me. The power that I have on you is to spare you, The malice towards you to forgive you. Live, And deal with others better. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Nobly doomed! We’ll learn our freeness of a son-in-law. Pardon’s the word to all. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | (to [[Posthumus]]) You holp us, sir, As you did mean indeed to be our brother. Joyed are we that you are. |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | Your servant, princes. (To [[Lucius]]) Good my lord of Rome, Call forth your soothsayer. As I slept, methought Great Jupiter, upon his eagle backed, Appeared to me with other spritely shows Of mine own kindred. When I waked I found This label on my bosom, whose containing Is so from sense in hardness that I can Make no collection of it. Let him show His skill in the construction. |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | Philharmonus. |
| SOOTHSAYER | Here, my good lord. |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | Read, and declare the meaning. |
| SOOTHSAYER | (reads the tablet) ‘Whenas a [[Lion|lion]]’s whelp shall, to himself unknown, without seeking find, and be embraced by a piece of tender air; and when from a stately cedar shall be lopped branches which, being dead many years, shall after revive, be jointed to the old stock, and freshly grow: then shall [[Posthumus]] end his miseries, Britain be fortunate and flourish in peace and plenty.’ Thou, Leonatus, art the [[Lion|lion]]’s whelp. The fit and apt construction of thy name, Being leo-natus, doth import so much. (To [[Cymbeline]]) The piece of tender air thy virtuous daughter, Which we call ‘mollis aer’; and ‘mollis aer’ |
| SOOTHSAYER | We term it ‘mulier’, (to [[Posthumus]]) which ‘mulier’ I divine Is this most constant wife, who even now, Answering the letter of the oracle, Unknown to you, unsought, were clipped about With this most tender air. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | This hath some seeming. |
| SOOTHSAYER | The lofty cedar, royal [[Cymbeline]], Personates thee, and thy lopped branches point Thy two sons forth, who, by [[Belarius]] stol’n, For many years thought dead, are now revived, To the majestic cedar joined, whose issue Promises Britain peace and plenty. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | [[Well]], My peace we will begin; and, Caius [[Lucius]], Although the victor, we submit to Caesar And to the Roman empire, promising To pay our wonted tribute, from the which We were dissuaded by our wicked [[Queen|queen]], Whom heavens in justice both on her and hers Have laid most heavy hand. |
| SOOTHSAYER | The fingers of the powers above do tune The harmony of this peace. The vision, Which I made known to [[Lucius]] ere the stroke Of this yet scarce-cold battle, at this instant Is full accomplished. For the Roman eagle, From south to west on wing soaring aloft, Lessened herself, and in the beams o‘th’ sun So vanished; which foreshowed our princely eagle Th’imperial Caesar should again unite His favour with the radiant [[Cymbeline]], Which shines here in the west. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--22_exchange_cymbeline" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — exchange (CYMBELINE) [22]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Laud we the gods, And let our crooked smokes climb to their nostrils From our blest altars. Publish we this peace To all our subjects. Set we forward, let A Roman and a British ensign wave Friendly together. So through Lud’s town march, And in the temple of great Jupiter Our peace we’ll ratify, seal it with feasts. Set on there. Never was a war did cease, Ere bloody hands were washed, with such a peace. Flourish. Exeunt in triumph |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_5--23_monologue_the" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 5 — monologue (THE) [23]" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| THE | [[King]]’s Men acted The Tempest before their patron, James I, at Whitehall on I November 1611. (It was also chosen for performance during the festivities for the [[Marriage|marriage]] of James’s daughter, Princess Elizabeth, to the Elector Palatine during the [[Winter|winter]] of 1612- 13.) Shakespeare’s play takes place on a [[Desert|desert]] island somewhere between Tunis and Naples; he derived some details of it from his reading of travel literature, including accounts of an expedition of nine ships taking five hundred colonists from Plymouth to Virginia, which set sail in May 1609. On 29 July the flagship, the Sea-Adventure, was wrecked by a [[Storm|storm]] on the coast of the Bermudas. She was presumed lost, but on 23 May 1610 those aboard her arrived safely in Jamestown, Virginia, having found shelter on the island of Bermuda, where they were able to build the pinnaces in which they completed their [[Journey|journey]]. Accounts of the voyage soon reached England; the last-written that Shakespeare seems to have known is a letter by William Strachey, who was on the Sea-Adventure, dated 15 July 1610; though it was not published until 1625, it circulated in manuscript. So it seems clear that Shakespeare wrote The Tempest during the later part of 1610or in 1611. It was first printed in the 1623 Folio, where it is the opening play. Though other items of Shakespeare’s reading—including both [[arthur|Arthur]] Golding’s [[Translation|translation]] and Ovid’s original Metamorphoses (closely echoed in Prospero’s farewell to his [[Magic|magic]]), John Florio’s [[Translation|translation]] of essays by Michel de Montaigne, and (less locally but no less pervasively) Virgil’s Aeneid—certainly fed Shakespeare’s [[Imagination|imagination]] as he wrote The Tempest, he appears to have devised the main plot himself. Many of its elements are based on the familiar stuff of [[Romance|romance]] literature: the long-past shipwreck after a perilous voyage of Prospero and his daughter [[Miranda]]; the shipwreck, depicted in the opening scene, of Prospero’s brother, [[Antonio]], with [[Alonso]], [[King]] of Naples, and others; the separation and estrangement of relatives—[[Antonio]] usurped Prospero’s dukedom, and [[Alonso]] believes his son, [[Ferdinand]], is drowned; the chaste [[Love|love]], subjected to trials, of the handsome [[Ferdinand]] and the beautiful [[Miranda]]; the influence of the [[Supernatural|supernatural]] exercised through Prospero’s [[Magic|magic]] powers; and the final reunions and reconciliations along with the happy conclusion of the [[Love|love]] affair. Shakespeare had employed such conventions from the beginning of his career in his comedies, and with especial concentration, shortly before he wrote The Tempest, in [[Pericles]], The [[Winter]]’s Tale, and [[Cymbeline]]. But whereas those plays unfold the events as they happen, taking us on a [[Journey|journey]] through [[Time|time]] and space, in The Tempest (as elsewhere only in The [[Comedy]] of Errors) Shakespeare gives us only the end of the story, concentrating the action into a few hours and locating it in a single place, but informing us about the past, as in the long, [[Romance|romance]]-type narrative (1.2) in which Prospero tells [[Miranda]] of her [[Childhood|childhood]]. The [[Supernatural|supernatural]], a strong presence in all Shakespeare’s late plays, is particularly pervasive in The Tempest; Prospero is a ‘white’ magician—a beneficent one—attended by the spirit [[Ariel]] and the sub-human [[Caliban]], two of Shakespeare’s most obviously symbolic characters; and a climax of the play is the supernaturally induced wedding [[Masque|masque]] that Prospero conjures up for the entertainment and edification of the young [[lovers]], and which vanishes as he remembers [[Caliban]]’s plot against his life. |

<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_2" data-title="Cymbeline — Act I, Scene 2" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Cloten]] and two Lords |
| FIRST LORD | Sir, I would advise you to shift a shirt. The violence of action hath made you reek as a sacrifice. Where air comes out, air comes in. There’s none abroad so wholesome as that you vent. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | If my shirt were bloody, then to shift it. Have I hurt him? |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) No, [[Faith|faith]], not so much as his patience. |
| FIRST LORD | Hurt him? His [[Body|body]]’s a passable carcass if he be not hurt. It is a thoroughfare for steel if he be not hurt. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) His steel was in debt—it went o’th’ backside the town. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | The [[Villain|villain]] would not stand me. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) No, but he fled forward still, toward your face. |
| FIRST LORD | Stand you? You have land enough of your own, but he added to your having, gave you some ground. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) As many inches as you have oceans. Puppies! |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | I would they had not come between us. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) So would I, till you had measured how long a [[Fool|fool]] you were upon the ground. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | And that she should [[Love|love]] this fellow and refuse me! |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) If it be a sin to make a true election, she is damned. |
| FIRST LORD | Sir, as I told you always, her [[Beauty|beauty]] and her brain go not together. She’s a good sign, but I have seen small reflection of her wit. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) She shines not upon fools lest the reflection should hurt her. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Come, I’ll to my chamber. Would there had been some hurt done. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) I wish not so, unless it had been the fall of an ass, which is no great hurt. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | (to Second Lord) You’ll go with us? |
| FIRST LORD | I’ll attend your lordship. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Nay, come, let’s go together. |
| SECOND LORD | [[Well]], my lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_5" data-title="Cymbeline — Act I, Scene 5" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |

| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Queen]], Ladies, and Cornelius, a doctor |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | Whiles yet the dew’s on ground, gather those [[Flowers|flowers]].<br>Make haste. Who has the note of them?<br>A LADY<br>I, madam. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | Dispatch. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Ladies |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | Now, Master Doctor, have you brought those drugs? |
| CORNELIUS | Pleaseth your highness, ay. Here they are, madam.<br>He gives her a box<br>But I beseech your grace, without offence—<br>My [[Conscience|conscience]] bids me ask—wherefore you have<br>Commanded of me these most poisonous compounds,<br>Which are the movers of a languishing [[Death|death]],<br>But though slow, deadly. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | I [[Wonder|wonder]], doctor,<br>Thou ask‘st me such a question. Have I not been<br>Thy pupil long? Hast thou not learned me how<br>To make perfumes, distil, preserve—yea, so<br>That our great [[King]] himself doth woo me oft<br>For my confections? Having thus far proceeded,<br>Unless thou think’st me devilish, is’t not meet<br>That I did amplify my judgement in<br>Other conclusions? I will try the forces<br>Of these thy compounds on such creatures as<br>We count not worth the hanging, but none human,<br>To try the vigour of them, and apply<br>Allayments to their act, and by them gather<br>Their several virtues and effects. |
| CORNELIUS | Your highness<br>Shall from this practice but make hard your heart.<br>Besides, the seeing these effects will be<br>Both noisome and infectious. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | O, content thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Pisanio |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | (Aside) Here comes a flattering rascal; upon him<br>Will I first work. He’s factor for his master,<br>And enemy to my son. (Aloud) How now, Pisanio?—<br>Doctor, your service for this [[Time|time]] is ended.<br>Take your own way. |
| CORNELIUS | (aside)<br>I do suspect you, madam.<br>But you shall do no harm. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | (to Pisanio)<br>Hark thee, a word. |
| CORNELIUS | (aside)<br>I do not like her. She doth think she has<br>Strange ling’ring poisons. I do know her spirit,<br>And will not trust one of her malice with<br>A drug of such damned [[Nature|nature]]. Those she has<br>Will stupefy and dull the sense a while,<br>Which first, perchance, she’ll prove on cats and [[dogs]],<br>Then afterward up higher; but there is<br>No danger in what show of [[Death|death]] it makes<br>More than the locking up the spirits a [[Time|time]],<br>To be more fresh, reviving. She is fooled<br>With a most false effect, and I the truer<br>So to be false with her. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | No further service, doctor,<br>Until I send for thee. |
| CORNELIUS | I humbly take my leave. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | (to Pisanio)<br>Weeps she still, sayst thou? Dost thou think in [[Time|time]]<br>She will not quench, and let instructions enter<br>Where folly now possesses? Do thou work.<br>When thou shalt bring me word she loves my son<br>I’ll tell thee on the instant thou art then<br>As great as is thy master—greater, for<br>His fortunes all lie speechless, and his name<br>Is at last gasp. Return he cannot, nor<br>Continue where he is. To shift his being<br>Is to exchange one misery with another,<br>And every day that comes comes to [[Decay|decay]]<br>A day’s work in him. What shalt thou expect<br>To be depender on a thing that leans,<br>Who cannot be new built nor has no friends<br>So much as but to prop him? |
| *(didascalia)* | She drops her box. He takes it up |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | Thou tak’st up<br>Thou know‘st not what; but take it for thy labour.<br>It is a thing I made which hath the [[King]]<br>Five times redeemed from [[Death|death]]. I do not know<br>What is more cordial. Nay, I prithee take it.<br>It is an earnest of a farther good<br>That I mean to thee. Tell thy mistress how<br>The case stands with her; do’t as from thyself.<br>Think what a chance thou changest on, but think<br>Thou hast thy mistress still; to boot, my son,<br>Who shall take notice of thee. I’ll move the [[King]]<br>To any shape of thy preferment, such<br>As thou’lt [[Desire|desire]]; and then myself, I chiefly,<br>That set thee on to this [[Desert|desert]], am bound<br>To load thy merit richly. Call my women.<br>Think on my words. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Pisanio |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | A sly and constant knave,<br>Not to be shaked; the agent for his master,<br>And the remembrancer of her to hold<br>The hand-fast to her lord. I have given him that<br>Which, if he take, shall quite unpeople her<br>Of liegers for her sweet, and which she after,<br>Except she bend her [[Humour|humour]], shall be assured<br>To taste of too. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Pisanio and Ladies |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | So, so; [[Well|well]] done, [[Well|well]] done.<br>The violets, cowslips, and the primroses<br>Bear to my closet. Fare thee [[Well|well]], Pisanio.<br>Think on my words, Pisanio. |
| PISANIO | And shall do. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Queen]] and Ladies |
| PISANIO | But when to my good lord I prove untrue,<br>I’ll choke mysetf—there’s all I’ll do for you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_1" data-title="Cymbeline — Act II, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Cloten]] and the two Lords |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Was there ever man had such luck? When I kissed the jack upon an upcast, to be hit away! I had a hundred pound on’t, and then a whoreson jackanapes must take me up for swearing, as if I borrowed mine oaths of him, and might not spend them at my pleasure. |
| FIRST LORD | What got he by that? You have broke his pate with your bowl. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) If his wit had been like him that broke it, it would have run all out. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | When a gentleman is disposed to swear it is not for any standers-by to curtail his oaths, ha? |
| SECOND LORD | No, my lord (aside)—nor crop the ears of them. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Whoreson dog! I give him satisfaction? Would he had been one of my rank. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) To have smelled like a [[Fool|fool]]. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | I am not vexed more at anything in th‘earth. A pox on’t, I had rather not be so noble as I am. They dare not fight with me because of the [[Queen]], my mother. Every jack-slave hath his bellyful of fighting, and I must go up and down like a cock that nobody can match. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) You are cock and capon too an you crow cock with your comb on. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Sayst thou? |
| SECOND LORD | It is not fit your lordship should undertake every companion that you give offence to. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | No, I know that, but it is fit I should commit offence to my inferiors. |
| SECOND LORD | Ay, it is fit for your lordship only. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Why, so I say. |
| FIRST LORD | Did you hear of a stranger that’s come to [[Court|court]] tonight? |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | A stranger, and I not know on’t? |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) He’s a strange fellow himself and knows it not. |
| FIRST LORD | There’s an Italian come, and, ’tis thought, one of Leonatus’ friends. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Leonatus? A banished rascal; and he’s another, whatsoever he be. Who told you of this stranger? |
| FIRST LORD | One of your lordship’s pages. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Is it fit I went to look upon him? Is there no derogation in’t? |
| SECOND LORD | You cannot derogate, my lord. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Not easily, I think. |
| SECOND LORD | (aside) You are a [[Fool|fool]] granted, therefore your issues, being foolish, do not derogate. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Come, I’ll go see this Italian. What I have lost today at bowls I’ll win tonight of him. Come, go. |
| SECOND LORD | I’ll attend your lordship. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Cloten]] and First Lord |
| SECOND LORD | That such a crafty devil as is his mother<br>Should yield the world this ass!—a woman that<br>Bears all down with her brain, and this her son<br>Cannot take two from twenty, for his heart,<br>And leave eighteen. Alas, poor princess,<br>Thou divine [[Innogen]], what thou endur‘st,<br>Betwixt a father by thy stepdame governed,<br>A mother hourly coining plots, a wooer<br>More hateful than the foul expulsion is<br>Of thy dear husband, than that horrid act<br>Of the [[divorce]] he’d make! The heavens hold firm<br>The walls of thy dear [[Honour|honour]], keep unshaked<br>That temple, thy fair mind, that thou mayst stand<br>T’enjoy thy banished lord and this great land! Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_2" data-title="Cymbeline — Act II, Scene 2" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | A trunk and arras. A bed is thrust forth with [[Innogen]] in it, reading a book. Enter to her [[Helen]], a lady |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | Who’s there? My woman [[Helen]]? |
| [[Helen|HELEN]] | Please you, madam. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | What hour is it? |
| [[Helen|HELEN]] | Almost midnight, madam. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | I have read three hours then. Mine eyes are weak.<br>Fold down the leaf where I have left. To bed.<br>Take not away the taper; leave it burning,<br>And if thou canst awake by four o’th’ [[Clock|clock]],<br>I prithee call me. [[Sleep]] hath seized me wholly. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Helen]] |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | To your protection I commend me, gods.<br>From fairies and the tempters of the [[Night|night]]<br>Guard me, beseech ye.<br>She sleeps.<br>Giacomo comes from the trunk |
| GIACOMO | The crickets sing, and man’s o‘er-laboured sense<br>Repairs itself by rest. Our [[Tarquin]] thus<br>Did softly press the rushes ere he wakened<br>The chastity he wounded. Cytherea,<br>How bravely thou becom’st thy bed! Fresh lily,<br>And whiter than the sheets! That I might touch,<br>But kiss, one kiss! Rubies unparagoned,<br>How dearly they do‘t! ’Tis her breathing that<br>Perfumes the chamber thus. The flame o‘th’ taper<br>Bows toward her, and would underpeep her lids,<br>To see th’enclosed lights, now canopied<br>Under these windows, white and azure-laced<br>With blue of heaven’s own tinct. But my design-<br>To note the chamber. I will write all down.<br>He writes in his tables<br>Such and such pictures, there the [[Window|window]], such<br>Th‘adornment of her bed, the arras, figures,<br>Why, such and such; and the contents o’th’ story.<br>Ah, but some natural notes about her [[Body|body]]<br>Above ten thousand meaner movables<br>Would testify t’enrich mine inventory.<br>O [[Sleep|sleep]], thou ape of [[Death|death]], lie dull upon her,<br>And be her sense but as a monument<br>Thus in a chapel lying. Come off, come off;<br>As slippery as the Gordian knot was hard.<br>He takes the bracelet from her arm<br>‘Tis mine, and this will witness outwardly,<br>As strongly as the [[Conscience|conscience]] does within,<br>To th’ madding of her lord. On her left breast<br>A mole, cinque-spotted, like the crimson drops<br>I’th’ [[Bottom|bottom]] of a cowslip. Here’s a voucher<br>Stronger than ever law could make. This secret<br>Will force him think I have picked the lock and<br>ta’en<br>The treasure of her [[Honour|honour]]. No more. To what end?<br>Why should I write this down that’s riveted,<br>Screwed to my [[Memory|memory]]? She hath been reading late,<br>The tale of Tereus. Here the leaf’s turned down<br>Where Philomel gave up. I have enough.<br>To th’ trunk again, and shut the [[spring]] of it.<br>Swift, swift, you dragons of the [[Night|night]], that dawning<br>May bare [[The Raven|the raven]]’s eye! I lodge in [[Fear|fear]].<br>Though this’ a heavenly angel, hell is here.<br>[[Clock]] strikes<br>One, two, three. [[Time]], [[Time|time]]! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit into the trunk. The bed and trunk are removed |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_2--scene_5" data-title="Cymbeline — Act II, Scene 5" data-chapter="Scene 5" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Posthumus]] |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | Is there no way for men to be, but women<br>Must be half-workers? We are bastards all,<br>And that most venerable man which I<br>Did call my father was I know not where<br>When I was stamped. Some coiner with his tools<br>Made me a counterfeit; yet my mother seemed<br>The Dian of that [[Time|time]]: so doth my wife<br>The nonpareil of this. O vengeance, vengeance!<br>Me of my lawful pleasure she restrained,<br>And prayed me oft forbearance; did it with<br>A pudency so rosy the sweet view on’t<br>Might [[Well|well]] have warmed old [[Saturn]]; that I thought<br>her<br>As chaste as unsunned [[Snow|snow]]. O all the devils!<br>This yellow Giacomo in an hour—was’t not?—<br>Or less—at first? Perchance he spoke not, but<br>Like a full-acorned boar, a German one,<br>Cried ‘O!’ and mounted; found no opposition<br>But what he looked for should oppose and she<br>Should from encounter guard. Could I find out<br>The woman’s part in me—for there’s no motion<br>That tends to vice in man but I affirm<br>It is the woman’s part; be it lying, note it,<br>The woman’s; flattering, hers; deceiving, hers;<br>Lust and rank thoughts, hers, hers; revenges, hers;<br>Ambitions, covetings, change of prides, disdain,<br>Nice [[Longing|longing]], slanders, [[Mutability|mutability]],<br>All faults that man can name, nay, that hell knows,<br>Why, hers in part or all, but rather all—<br>For even to vice<br>They are not constant, but are changing still<br>One vice but of a minute old for one<br>Not half so old as that. I’ll write against them,<br>Detest them, curse them, yet ’tis greater skill<br>In a true hate to pray they have their will.<br>The very devils cannot plague them better. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | 3.1 Flourish. Enter in state [[Cymbeline]], the [[Queen]], [[Cloten]], and lords at one door, and at another, Caius [[Lucius]] and attendants |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Now say, what would Augustus Caesar with us? |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | When [[Julius Caesar]]—whose remembrance yet<br>Lives in men’s eyes, and will to ears and tongues<br>Be theme and hearing ever—was in this Britain<br>And conquered it, Cassibelan, thine uncle,<br>Famous in Caesar’s praises no whit less<br>Than in his feats deserving it, for him<br>And his succession granted Rome a tribute,<br>Yearly three thousand pounds, which by thee lately<br>Is left untendered. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | And, to kill the marvel,<br>Shall be so ever. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | There will be many Caesars<br>Ere such another Julius. Britain’s a world<br>By itself, and we will nothing pay<br>For wearing our own noses. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | That opportunity<br>Which then they had to take from ‘s, to resume<br>We have again. Remember, sir, my liege,<br>The kings your ancestors, together with<br>The natural bravery of your isle, which stands<br>As Neptune’s park, ribbed and paled in<br>With banks unscalable and roaring waters,<br>With sands that will not bear your enemies’ boats,<br>But suck them up to th’ topmast. A kind of conquest<br>Caesar made here, but made not here his brag<br>Of ‘came and saw and overcame’. With shame—<br>The first that ever touched him—he was carried<br>From off our coast, twice beaten; and his shipping,<br>Poor ignorant baubles, on our terrible seas<br>Like eggshells moved upon their surges, cracked<br>As easily ’gainst our rocks; for joy whereof<br>The famed Cassibelan, who was once at point—<br>O giglot fortune!—to master Caesar’s [[Sword|sword]],<br>Made Lud’s town with rejoicing fires bright,<br>And Britons strut with [[Courage|courage]]. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | Come, there’s no more tribute to be paid. Our kingdom is stronger than it was at that [[Time|time]], and, as I said, there is no more such Caesars. Other of them may have crooked noses, but to owe such straight arms, none. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Son, let your mother end. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | We have yet many among us can grip as hard as Cassibelan. I do not say I am one, but I have a hand. Why tribute? Why should we pay tribute? If Caesar can hide the sun from us with a blanket, or put the [[Moon|moon]] in his pocket, we will pay him tribute for light; else, sir, no more tribute, pray you now. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | (to [[Lucius]]) You must know,<br>Till the injurious Romans did extort<br>This tribute from us we were free. Caesar’s [[Ambition|ambition]],<br>Which swelled so much that it did almost stretch<br>The sides o‘th’ world, against all colour here<br>Did put the yoke upon ’s, which to shake off<br>Becomes a warlike people, whom we reckon<br>Ourselves to be. We do say then to Caesar,<br>Our ancestor was that Mulmutius which<br>Ordained our laws, whose use the [[Sword|sword]] of Caesar<br>Hath too much mangled, whose repair and franchise<br>Shall by the power we hold be our good deed,<br>Though Rome be therefore angry. Mulmutius made<br>our laws,<br>Who was the first of Britain which did put<br>His brows within a golden [[Crown|crown]] and called<br>Himself a [[King|king]]. |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | I am sorry, [[Cymbeline]],<br>That I am to pronounce Augustus Caesar—<br>Caesar, that hath more kings his servants than<br>Thyself domestic officers—thine enemy.<br>Receive it from me, then: war and confusion<br>In Caesar’s name pronounce I ’gainst thee. Look<br>For fury not to be resisted. Thus defied,<br>I thank thee for myself. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Thou art welcome, Caius.<br>Thy Caesar knighted me; my youth I spent<br>Much under him; of him I gathered [[Honour|honour]],<br>Which he to seek of me again perforce<br>Behoves me keep at utterance. I am perfect<br>That the Pannonians and Dalmatians for<br>Their liberties are now in arms, a precedent<br>Which not to read would show the Britons cold;<br>So Caesar shall not find them. |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | Let proof speak. |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | His majesty bids you welcome. Make pastime with us a day or two or longer. If you seek us afterwards in other terms, you shall find us in our salt-[[Water|water]] girdle. If you beat us out of it, it is yours; if you fall in the adventure, our crows shall fare the better for you; and there’s an end. |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | So, sir. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | I know your master’s pleasure, and he mine.<br>All the remain is ‘Welcome’. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_3--scene_2" data-title="Cymbeline — Act III, Scene 2" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Pisanio, reading of a letter |
| PISANIO | How? Of adultery? Wherefore write you not<br>What [[Monster|monster]]’s her accuser? Leonatus,<br>O master, what a strange infection<br>Is fall’n into thy ear! What false Italian,<br>As poisonous tongued as handed, hath prevailed<br>On thy too ready hearing? Disloyal? No.<br>She’s punished for her [[Truth|truth]], and undergoes,<br>More goddess-like than wife-like, such assaults<br>As would take in some virtue. O my master,<br>Thy mind to hers is now as low as were<br>Thy fortunes. How? That I should murder her,<br>Upon the [[Love|love]] and [[Truth|truth]] and vows which I<br>Have made to thy command? I her? Her [[Blood|blood]]?<br>If it be so to do good service, never<br>Let me be counted serviceable. How look I,<br>That I should seem to lack humanity<br>So much as this fact comes to? (Reads) ‘Do’t. The letter<br>That I have sent her, by her own command<br>Shall give thee opportunity.’ O damned paper,<br>Black as the ink that’s on thee! Senseless bauble,<br>Art thou a fedary for this act, and look’st<br>So virgin-like without? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Innogen]] |
| PISANIO | Lo, here she comes.<br>I am ignorant in what I am commanded. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | How now, Pisanio? |
| PISANIO | Madam, here is a letter from my lord. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | Who, thy lord that is my lord, Leonatus?<br>O learned indeed were that astronomer<br>That knew the [[Stars|stars]] as I his characters—<br>He’d lay the future open. You good gods,<br>Let what is here contained relish of [[Love|love]],<br>Of my lord’s health, of his content—yet not<br>That we two are asunder; let that grieve him.<br>Some griefs are med’cinable; that is one of them,<br>For it doth physic [[Love|love]]—of his content<br>All but in that. Good wax, thy leave. Blest be<br>You [[Bees|bees]] that make these locks of counsel! [[lovers|Lovers]]<br>And men in dangerous bonds pray not alike;<br>Though forfeiters you cast in [[Prison|prison]], yet<br>You clasp young Cupid’s tables. Good news, gods!<br>She opens and reads the letter<br>’Justice and your father’s wrath, should he take me in<br>his dominion, could not be so cruel to me as you, O<br>the dearest of creatures, would even renew me with<br>your eyes. Take notice that I am in Cambria, at Milford<br>Haven. What your own [[Love|love]] will out of this advise you,<br>follow. So he wishes you all happiness, that remains<br>loyal to his vow, and your increasing in [[Love|love]],<br>Leonatus [[Posthumus]].’<br>O for a [[Horse|horse]] with wings! Hear‘st thou, Pisanio?<br>He is at Milford Haven. Read, and tell me<br>How far ’tis thither. If one of mean affairs<br>May plod it in a week, why may not I<br>Glide thither in a day? Then, true Pisanio,<br>Who long‘st like me to see thy lord, who long’st—<br>O let me bate—but not like me—yet long‘st<br>But in a fainter kind—O, not like me,<br>For mine’s beyond beyond; say, and speak thick—<br>[[Love]]’s counsellor should fill the bores of hearing,<br>To th’ smothering of the sense—how far it is<br>To this same blessèd Milford. And by th’ way<br>Tell me how Wales was made so happy as<br>T’inherit such a haven. But first of all,<br>How we may steal from hence; and for the gap<br>That we shall make in [[Time|time]] from our hence-going<br>Till our return, to excuse; but first, how get hence.<br>Why should excuse be born or ere begot?<br>We’ll talk of that hereafter. Prithee speak,<br>How many score of miles may we [[Well|well]] ride<br>’Twixt hour and hour? |
| PISANIO | One score ’twixt sun and sun,<br>Madam, ’s enough for you, and too much too. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | Why, one that rode to ’s execution, man,<br>Could never go so slow. I have heard of riding wagers<br>Where horses have been nimbler than the sands<br>That run i‘th’ [[Clock|clock]]’s behalf. But this is [[Fool|fool]]’ry.<br>Go bid my woman feign a sickness, say<br>She’ll [[Home|home]] to her father; and provide me presently<br>A riding-suit no costlier than would fit<br>A franklin’s housewife. |
| PISANIO | Madam, you’re best consider. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | I see before me, man. Nor here, nor here,<br>Nor what ensues, but have a fog in them<br>That I cannot look through. Away, I prithee,<br>Do as I bid thee. There’s no more to say:<br>Accessible is none but Milford way. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_3--scene_3" data-title="Cymbeline — Act III, Scene 3" data-chapter="Scene 3" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Belarius]], followed by [[Guiderius]] and [[Arviragus]], from a [[Cave|cave]] in the woods |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | A goodly day not to keep [[house]] with such<br>Whose roof’s as low as ours. Stoop, boys; this gate<br>Instructs you how t‘adore the heavens, and bows you<br>To a morning’s holy office. The gates of monarchs<br>Are arched so high that giants may jet through<br>And keep their impious turbans on without<br>Good morrow to the sun. Hail, thou fair heaven!<br>We [[house]] i’th’ rock, yet use thee not so hardly<br>As prouder livers do. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Hail, heaven! |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | Hail, heaven! |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Now for our mountain sport. Up to yon [[Hill|hill]],<br>Your legs are young; I’ll tread these flats. Consider,<br>When you above perceive me like a crow,<br>That it is place which lessens and sets off,<br>And you may then revolve what tales I have told you<br>Of courts, of princes, of the tricks in war;<br>That service is not service, so being done,<br>But being so allowed. To apprehend thus<br>Draws us a profit from all things we see,<br>And often to our comfort shall we find<br>The sharded beetle in a safer hold<br>Than is the full-winged eagle. O, this life<br>Is nobler than attending for a check,<br>Richer than doing nothing for a bauble,<br>Prouder than rustling in unpaid-for silk;<br>Such gain the cap of him that makes ’em fine,<br>Yet keeps his book uncrossed. No life to ours. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Out of your proof you speak. We, poor unfledged,<br>Have never winged from view o’th’ nest, nor know<br>not<br>What air’s from [[Home|home]]. Haply this life is best,<br>If quiet life be best; sweeter to you<br>That have a sharper known; [[Well|well]] corresponding<br>With your stiff age, but unto us it is<br>A cell of ignorance, travelling abed,<br>A [[Prison|prison]] for a debtor, that not dares<br>To stride a limit. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | (to [[Belarius]]) What should we speak of<br>When we are old as you? When we shall hear<br>The rain and [[Wind|wind]] beat dark December, how,<br>In this our pinching [[Cave|cave]], shall we discourse<br>The freezing hours away? We have seen nothing.<br>We are beastly: subtle as the fox for prey,<br>Like warlike as the wolf for what we eat.<br>Our valour is to chase what flies; our cage<br>We make a choir, as doth the prisoned bird,<br>And sing our bondage freely. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | How you speak!<br>Did you but know the [[City|city]]’s usuries,<br>And felt them knowingly; the art o‘th’ [[Court|court]],<br>As hard to leave as keep, whose top to climb<br>Is certain falling, or so slipp’ry that<br>The [[Fear|fear]]’s as bad as falling; the toil o‘th’ war,<br>A [[Pain|pain]] that only seems to seek out danger<br>I’th’ name of fame and [[Honour|honour]], which dies i‘th’ search<br>And hath as oft a sland’rous epitaph<br>As record of fair act; nay, many times<br>Doth ill deserve by doing [[Well|well]]; what’s worse,<br>Must curtsy at the censure. O boys, this story<br>The world may read in me. My [[Body|body]]’s marked<br>With Roman swords, and my report was once<br>First with the best of note. [[Cymbeline]] loved me,<br>And when a soldier was the theme my name<br>Was not far off. Then was I as a tree<br>Whose boughs did bend with fruit; but in one [[Night|night]]<br>A [[Storm|storm]] or robbery, call it what you will,<br>Shook down my mellow hangings, nay, my [[Leaves|leaves]],<br>And left me bare to weather. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Uncertain favour! |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | My fault being nothing, as I have told you oft,<br>But that two villains, whose false oaths prevailed<br>Before my perfect [[Honour|honour]], swore to [[Cymbeline]]<br>I was confederate with the Romans. So<br>Followed my banishment, and this twenty years<br>This rock and these demesnes have been my world,<br>Where I have lived at honest [[Freedom|freedom]], paid<br>More pious debts to heaven than in all<br>The fore-end of my [[Time|time]]. But up to th’ [[Mountains|mountains]]!<br>This is not hunter’s language. He that strikes<br>The venison first shall be the lord o’th’ feast,<br>To him the other two shall minister,<br>And we will [[Fear|fear]] no [[Poison|poison]] which attends<br>In place of greater state. I’ll meet you in the valleys. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Guiderius]] and [[Arviragus]] |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | How hard it is to hide the sparks of [[Nature|nature]]!<br>These boys know little they are sons to th’ [[King]],<br>Nor [[Cymbeline]] [[Dreams|dreams]] that they are alive.<br>They think they are mine, and though trained up<br>thus meanly<br>I‘th’ [[Cave|cave]] wherein they bow, their thoughts do hit<br>The roofs of palaces, and [[Nature|nature]] prompts them<br>In simple and low things to prince it much<br>Beyond the trick of others. This Polydore,<br>The heir of [[Cymbeline]] and Britain, who<br>The [[King]] his father called [[Guiderius]]—Jove,<br>When on my three-foot stool I sit and tell<br>The warlike feats I have done, his spirits fly out<br>Into my story: say ‘Thus mine enemy fell,<br>And thus I set my foot on ’s neck’, even then<br>The princely [[Blood|blood]] flows in his cheek, he sweats,<br>Strains his young nerves, and puts himself in posture<br>That acts my words. The younger brother, Cadwal,<br>Once [[Arviragus]], in as like a figure<br>Strikes life into my speech, and shows much more<br>His own conceiving. |
| *(didascalia)* | A hunting-horn sounds |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Hark, the game is roused!<br>O [[Cymbeline]], heaven and my [[Conscience|conscience]] knows<br>Thou didst unjustly banish me, whereon<br>At three and two years old I stole these babes,<br>Thinking to bar thee of succession as<br>Thou reft’st me of my lands. Euriphile,<br>Thou wast their [[Nurse|nurse]]; they took thee for their<br>mother,<br>And every day do [[Honour|honour]] to her [[Grave|grave]].<br>Myself, [[Belarius]], that am Morgan called,<br>They take for natural father. |
| *(didascalia)* | A hunting-horn sounds |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | The game is up. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_3--scene_6" data-title="Cymbeline — Act III, Scene 6" data-chapter="Scene 6" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Innogen]], dressed as a man, before the [[Cave|cave]] |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | I see a man’s life is a tedious one.<br>I have tired myself, and for two nights together<br>Have made the ground my bed. I should be sick,<br>But that my resolution helps me. Milford,<br>When from the mountain-top Pisanio showed thee,<br>Thou wast within a ken. O Jove, I think<br>Foundations fly the wretched—such, I mean,<br>Where they should be relieved. Two beggars told me<br>I could not miss my way. Will poor folks lie,<br>That have afflictions on them, knowing ‘tis<br>A punishment or trial? Yes. No [[Wonder|wonder]],<br>When rich ones scarce tell true. To lapse in fullness<br>Is sorer than to lie for need, and falsehood<br>Is worse in kings than beggars. My dear lord,<br>Thou art one o’th’ false ones. Now I think on thee<br>My hunger’s gone, but even before I was<br>At point to sink for food. But what is this?<br>Here is a path to’t. ‘Tis some savage hold.<br>I were best not call; I dare not call; yet famine,<br>Ere clean it o’erthrow [[Nature|nature]], makes it valiant.<br>Plenty and peace breeds cowards, hardness ever<br>Of hardiness is mother. Ho! Who’s here?<br>If anything that’s civil, speak; if savage,<br>Take or lend. Ho! No answer? Then I’ll enter.<br>Best draw my [[Sword|sword]], and if mine enemy<br>But [[Fear|fear]] the [[Sword|sword]] like me he’ll scarcely look on’t.<br>Such a foe, good heavens! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit into the [[Cave|cave]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Belarius]], [[Guiderius]], and [[Arviragus]] |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | You, Polydore, have proved best woodman and<br>Are master of the feast. Cadwal and I<br>Will play the cook and servant; ‘tis our match.<br>The sweat of industry would dry and die<br>But for the end it works to. Come, our stomachs<br>Will make what’s homely savoury. Weariness<br>Can snore upon the flint when resty sloth<br>Finds the down pillow hard. Now peace be here,<br>Poor [[house]], that keep’st thyself. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | I am throughly weary. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | I am weak with toil yet strong in appetite. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | There is cold meat i’th’ [[Cave|cave]]. We’ll browse on that<br>Whilst what we have killed be cooked. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | (looking into the [[Cave|cave]]) Stay, come not in.<br>But that it eats our victuals I should think<br>Here were a fairy. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | What’s the matter, sir? |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | By Jupiter, an angel—or, if not,<br>An earthly paragon. Behold divineness<br>No elder than a boy. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Innogen]] from the [[Cave|cave]], dressed as a man |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | Good masters, harm me not.<br>Before I entered here I called, and thought<br>To have begged or bought what I have took. Good<br>[[Truth|truth]],<br>I have stol’n naught, nor would not, though I had<br>found<br>Gold strewed i’th’ floor. Here’s money for my meat.<br>I would have left it on the board so soon<br>As I had made my meal, and parted<br>With prayers for the provider. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Money, youth? |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | All gold and silver rather turn to dirt,<br>As ’tis no better reckoned but of those<br>Who worship dirty gods. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | I see you’re angry.<br>Know, if you kill me for my fault, I should<br>Have died had I not made it. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Whither bound? |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | To Milford Haven. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | What’s your name? |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | Fidele, sir. I have a kinsman who<br>Is bound for [[Italy]]. He embarked at Milford,<br>To whom being going, almost spent with hunger,<br>I am fall’n in this offence. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Prithee, fair youth,<br>Think us no churls, nor measure our good minds<br>By this rude place we live in. [[Well]] encountered.<br>’Tis almost [[Night|night]]. You shall have better cheer<br>Ere you depart, and thanks to stay and eat it.<br>Boys, bid him welcome. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Were you a woman, youth,<br>I should woo hard but be your groom in honesty,<br>Ay, bid for you as I’d buy. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | I’ll make’t my comfort<br>He is a man, I’ll [[Love|love]] him as my brother.<br>(To [[Innogen]]) And such a welcome as I’d give to him<br>After long absence, such is yours. Most welcome.<br>Be sprightly, for you fall ’mongst friends. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | ’Mongst friends<br>If brothers. (Aside) Would it had been so that they<br>Had been my father’s sons. Then had my price<br>Been less, and so more equal ballasting<br>To thee, [[Posthumus]].<br>The three men speak apart |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | He wrings at some distress. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Would I could free’t. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | Or I, whate’er it be,<br>What [[Pain|pain]] it cost, what danger. Gods! |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Hark, boys.<br>They whisper |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | (aside) Great men<br>That had a [[Court|court]] no bigger than this [[Cave|cave]],<br>That did attend themselves and had the virtue<br>Which their own [[Conscience|conscience]] sealed them, laying by<br>That nothing-gift of differing multitudes,<br>Could not outpeer these twain. Pardon me, gods,<br>I’d change my sex to be companion with them,<br>Since Leonatus’ false. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | It shall be so.<br>Boys, we’ll go dress our hunt. Fair youth, come in.<br>Discourse is heavy, fasting. When we have supped<br>We’ll mannerly demand thee of thy story,<br>So far as thou wilt speak it. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Pray draw near. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | The [[Night|night]] to th’ owl and morn to th’ lark less welcome. |
| [[Innogen|INNOGEN]] | Thanks, sir. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | I pray draw near. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt into the [[Cave|cave]] |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_3--scene_7" data-title="Cymbeline — Act III, Scene 7" data-chapter="Scene 7" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter two Roman Senators, and Tribunes |
| FIRST SENATOR | This is the tenor of the Emperor’s writ:<br>That since the common men are now in action<br>‘Gainst the Pannonians and Dalmatians,<br>And that the legions now in Gallia are<br>Full weak to undertake our wars against<br>The fall’n-off Britons, that we do incite<br>The gentry to this business. He creates<br>[[Lucius]] pro-consul, and to you the tribunes,<br>For this immediate levy, he commends<br>His absolute commission. Long live Caesar!<br>A TRIBUNE<br>Is [[Lucius]] general of the forces? |
| SECOND SENATOR | Ay.<br>A TRIBUNE<br>Remaining now in Gallia? |
| FIRST SENATOR | With those legions<br>Which I have spoke of, whereunto your levy<br>Must be supplyant. The words of your commission<br>Will tie you to the numbers and the [[Time|time]]<br>Of their dispatch.<br>A TRIBUNE<br>We will discharge our duty. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_4--scene_1" data-title="Cymbeline — Act IV, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Cloten]], in [[Posthumus]]’ suit |
| [[Cloten|CLOTEN]] | I am near to th’ place where they should meet, if Pisanio have mapped it truly. How fit his garments serve me! Why should his mistress, who was made by him that made the tailor, not be fit too?—the rather—saving reverence of the word—for ’tis said a woman’s fitness comes by fits. Therein I must play the workman. I dare speak it to myself, for it is not vainglory for a man and his glass to confer in his own chamber. I mean the lines of my [[Body|body]] are as [[Well|well]] drawn as his: no less young, more strong, not beneath him in fortunes, beyond him in the advantage of the [[Time|time]], above him in birth, alike conversant in general services, and more remarkable in single oppositions. Yet this imperceiverant thing loves him in my despite. What [[Mortality|mortality]] is! [[Posthumus]], thy head which now is growing upon thy shoulders shall within this hour be off, thy mistress enforced, thy garments cut to pieces before thy face; and all this done, spurn her [[Home|home]] to her father, who may haply be a little angry for my so rough usage; but my mother, having power of his testiness, shall turn all into my commendations. My [[Horse|horse]] is tied up safe. Out, [[Sword|sword]], and to a sore purpose! Fortune, put them into my hand. This is the very description of their meeting-place, and the fellow dares not deceive me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_4--scene_3" data-title="Cymbeline — Act IV, Scene 3" data-chapter="Scene 3" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Cymbeline]], Lords, and Pisanio |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Again, and bring me word how ’tis with her. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one or more |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | A fever with the absence of her son,<br>A [[Madness|madness]] of which her life’s in danger-heavens,<br>How deeply you at once do touch me! [[Innogen]],<br>The great part of my comfort, gone; my [[Queen|queen]]<br>Upon a desperate bed, and in a [[Time|time]]<br>When fearful wars point at me; her son gone,<br>So needful for this present! It strikes me past<br>The [[Hope|hope]] of comfort. (To Pisanio) But for thee, fellow,<br>Who needs must know of her departure and<br>Dost seem so ignorant, we’ll enforce it from thee<br>By a sharp torture. |
| PISANIO | Sir, my life is yours.<br>I humbly set it at your will. But for my mistress,<br>I nothing know where she remains, why gone,<br>Nor when she purposes return. Beseech your<br>highness,<br>Hold me your loyal servant.<br>A LORD<br>Good my liege,<br>The day that she was missing he was here.<br>I dare be bound he’s true, and shall perform<br>All parts of his subjection loyally. For [[Cloten]],<br>There wants no diligence in seeking him,<br>And will no [[Doubt|doubt]] be found. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | The [[Time|time]] is troublesome.<br>(To Pisanio) We’ll slip you for a season, but our [[Jealousy|jealousy]]<br>Does yet depend.<br>A LORD<br>So please your majesty,<br>The Roman legions, all from Gallia drawn,<br>Are landed on your coast with a supply<br>Of Roman gentlemen by the senate sent. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | Now for the counsel of my son and [[Queen|queen]]!<br>I am amazed with matter.<br>A LORD<br>Good my liege,<br>Your preparation can affront no less<br>Than what you hear of. Come more, for more you’re<br>ready.<br>The want is but to put those powers in motion<br>That long to move. |
| [[Cymbeline|CYMBELINE]] | I thank you. Let’s withdraw,<br>And meet the [[Time|time]] as it seeks us. We [[Fear|fear]] not<br>What can from [[Italy]] annoy us, but<br>We grieve at chances here. Away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Cymbeline]] and Lords |
| PISANIO | I heard no letter from my master since<br>I wrote him [[Innogen]] was slain. ‘Tis strange.<br>Nor hear I from my mistress, who did promise<br>To yield me often tidings. Neither know I<br>What is betid to [[Cloten]], but remain<br>Perplexed in all. The heavens still must work.<br>Wherein I am false I am honest; not true, to be true.<br>These present wars shall find I [[Love|love]] my country<br>Even to the note o’th’ [[King]], or I’ll fall in them.<br>All other doubts, by [[Time|time]] let them be cleared:<br>Fortune brings in some boats that are not steered. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_4--scene_4" data-title="Cymbeline — Act IV, Scene 4" data-chapter="Scene 4" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Belarius]], [[Guiderius]], and [[Arviragus]] |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | The noise is round about us. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Let us from it. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | What pleasure, sir, find we in life to lock it<br>From action and adventure? |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Nay, what [[Hope|hope]]<br>Have we in hiding us? This way the Romans<br>Must or for Britains slay us, or receive us<br>For barbarous and unnatural revolts<br>During their use, and slay us after. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Sons,<br>We’ll higher to the [[Mountains|mountains]]; there secure us.<br>To the [[King]]’s party there’s no going. Newness<br>Of [[Cloten]]’s [[Death|death]]-we being not known, not mustered<br>Among the bands—may drive us to a render<br>Where we have lived, and so extort from ’s that<br>Which we have done, whose answer would be [[Death|death]]<br>Drawn on with torture. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | This is, sir, a [[Doubt|doubt]]<br>In such a [[Time|time]] nothing becoming you<br>Nor satisfying us. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | It is not likely<br>That when they hear the Roman horses neigh,<br>Behold their quartered files, have both their eyes<br>And ears so cloyed importantly as now,<br>That they will waste their [[Time|time]] upon our note,<br>To know from whence we are. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | O, I am known<br>Of many in the army. Many years,<br>Though [[Cloten]] then but young, you see, not wore him<br>From my remembrance. And besides, the [[King]]<br>Hath not deserved my service nor your loves,<br>Who find in my [[Exile|exile]] the want of breeding,<br>The certainty of this hard life; aye hopeless<br>To have the courtesy your cradle promised,<br>But to be still hot [[Summer|summer]]’s tanlings, and<br>The shrinking slaves of [[Winter|winter]]. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | Than be so,<br>Better to cease to be. Pray, sir, to th‘army.<br>I and my brother are not known; yourself<br>So out of thought, and thereto so o’ergrown,<br>Cannot be questioned. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | By this sun that shines,<br>I’ll thither. What thing is’t that I never<br>Did see man die, scarce ever looked on [[Blood|blood]]<br>But that of coward hares, hot goats, and venison,<br>Never bestrid a [[Horse|horse]] save one that had<br>A rider like myself, who ne’er wore rowel<br>Nor iron on his heel! I am ashamed<br>To look upon the holy sun, to have<br>The benefit of his blest beams, remaining<br>So long a poor unknown. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | By heavens, I’ll go.<br>If you will bless me, sir, and give me leave,<br>I’ll take the better care; but if you will not,<br>The hazard therefore due fall on me by<br>The hands of Romans. |
| [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] | So say I, amen. |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | No reason I, since of your lives you set<br>So slight a valuation, should reserve<br>My cracked one to more care. Have with you, boys!<br>If in your country wars you chance to die,<br>That is my bed, too, lads, and there I’ll lie.<br>Lead, lead. (Aside) The [[Time|time]] seems long. Their [[Blood|blood]]<br>thinks scorn<br>Till it fly out and show them princes born. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_1" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Posthumus]], dressed as an Italian gentleman, carrying a bloody cloth |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | Yea, bloody cloth, I’ll keep thee, for I once wished<br>Thou shouldst be coloured thus. You married ones,<br>If each of you should take this course, how many<br>Must murder wives much better than themselves<br>For wrying but a little! O Pisanio,<br>Every good servant does not all commands,<br>No bond but to do just ones. Gods, if you<br>Should have ta‘en vengeance on my faults, I never<br>Had lived to put on this; so had you saved<br>The noble [[Innogen]] to repent, and struck<br>Me, wretch, more worth your vengeance. But alack,<br>You snatch some hence for little faults; that’s [[Love|love]],<br>To have them fall no more. You some permit<br>To second ills with ills, each elder worse,<br>And make them dread ill, to the doer’s thrift.<br>But [[Innogen]] is your own. Do your blest wills,<br>And make me blest to obey. I am brought hither<br>Among th’Italian gentry, and to fight<br>Against my lady’s kingdom. ’Tis enough<br>That, Britain, I have killed thy mistress-piece;<br>I’ll give no wound to thee. Therefore, good heavens,<br>Hear patiently my purpose. I’ll disrobe me<br>Of these Italian weeds, and suit myself<br>As does a Briton peasant. |
| *(didascalia)* | He disrobes himself |
| [[Posthumus|POSTHUMUS]] | So I’ll fight<br>Against the part I come with; so I’ll die<br>For thee, O [[Innogen]], even for whom my life<br>Is every breath a [[Death|death]]; and, thus unknown,<br>Pitied nor hated, to the face of peril<br>Myself I’ll dedicate. Let me make men know<br>More valour in me than my habits show.<br>Gods, put the strength o‘th’ Leonati in me.<br>To shame the guise o’th’ world, I will begin<br>The fashion-less without and more within. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | 5.2 A march. Enter [[Lucius]], Giacomo, and the Roman army at one door, and the Briton army at another, Leonatus [[Posthumus]] following like a poor soldier. They march over and go out. Alarums. Then enter again in skirmish Giacomo and [[Posthumus]]: he vanquisheth and disarmeth Giacomo, and then [[Leaves|leaves]] him |
| GIACOMO | The heaviness and [[Guilt|guilt]] within my bosom<br>Takes off my manhood. I have belied a lady,<br>The princess of this country, and the air on’t<br>Revengingly enfeebles me; or could this carl,<br>A very drudge of [[Nature|nature]]’s, have subdued me<br>In my profession? Knighthoods and honours borne<br>As I wear mine are titles but of scorn.<br>If that thy gentry, Britain, go before<br>This lout as he exceeds our lords, the odds<br>Is that we scarce are men and you are gods. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_5--scene_3" data-title="Cymbeline — Act V, Scene 3" data-chapter="Scene 3" data-kind="scene"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | The battle continues. Alarums. Excursions. The trumpets sound a retreat. The Britons fly, [[Cymbeline]] is taken. Then enter to his rescue [[Belarius]], [[Guiderius]], and [[Arviragus]] |
| [[Belarius|BELARIUS]] | Stand, stand, we have th’advantage of the ground.<br>The lane is guarded. Nothing routs us but<br>The villainy of our fears. |
| [[Guiderius|GUIDERIUS]] | and [[Arviragus|ARVIRAGUS]] Stand, stand, and fight. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Posthumus]] like a poor soldier, and seconds the Britons. They rescue [[Cymbeline]] and exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 5.4 The trumpets sound a retreat, then enter [[Lucius]], Giacomo, and [[Innogen]] |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | (to [[Innogen]])<br>Away, boy, from the troops, and save thyself;<br>For friends kill friends, and the disorder’s such<br>As war were hoodwinked. |
| GIACOMO | ’Tis their fresh supplies. |
| [[Lucius|LUCIUS]] | It is a day turned strangely. Or betimes<br>Let’s reinforce, or fly. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
